[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 1.7 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"aeed21","wall_time_seconds":0.2626069,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":2805,"output":"# Doing well in your courses\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://cs.stanford.edu/people/karpathy/advice.html\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: unknown\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Doing well in your courses\\r\\n\\r\\na guide by Andrej Karpathy\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\nHere is some advice I would give to younger students if they wish to do well in their undergraduate courses.  \\r\\n  \\r\\nHaving been tested for many years of my life (with pretty good results), here are some rules of thumb that I feel helped me:\\r\\n\\r\\n## GENERAL\\r\\n\\r\\n**All-nighters are not worth it.**Sleep does wonders. Optimal sleep time for me is around 7.5 hours, with an absolute minimum of around 4hrs.  \\r\\nIt has happened to me several times that I was stuck on some problem for an hour in the night, but was able to solve it in 5 minutes in the morning. I feel like the brain \\"commits\\" a lot of shaky short-term memories to stable long-term memories during the night. I try to start studying for any big tests well in advance (several days), even if for short periods of time, to maximize the number of nights that my brain gets for the material.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Attend tutorials or review sessions**.  \\r\\nEven if they are bad. The fact that they get you to think about the material is what counts. If its too boring, you can always work on something else. Remember that you can also try to attend a different tutorial with a different TA.\\r\\n\\r\\n## TESTS: PREPARATION\\r\\n\\r\\n**Considering the big picture and organisation is the key.**   \\r\\nCreate schedule of study, even if you dont stick to it. For me this usually involves getting an idea of everything I need to know and explicitly writing it down in terms of bullet points. Consider all points carefully and think about how long it will take you to get them down. If you don\'t do this, there is a tendency to spend too much time on beginning of material and then skim through the (most important) later material due to lack of time.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always try to look at previous tests BEFORE starting to study**.  \\r\\nEspecially if the past tests were written by the same professor. This will give you strong hints about how you should study. Every professor has a different evaluation style. Don\'t actually attempt to complete the questions in the beginning, but take careful note of the type of questions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Reading and understanding IS NOT the same as replicating the content.**Even I often make this mistake still: You read a formula/derivation/proof in the book and it makes perfect sense. Now close the book and try to write it down. You will find that this process is completely different and it will amaze you that many times you won\'t actually be able to do this! Somehow the two things use different parts of the memory. Make it a point to make sure that you can actually write down the most important bits, and that you can re-derive them at will. Feynman famously [knew this](http://www.quora.com/What-did-Richard-Feynman-mean-when-he-said-What-I-cannot-create-I-do-not-understand) very well.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always try to collaborate with others, but near the end.**Study alone first because in the early stages of studying others can only serve as a distraction. But near the end get together with others: they will often point out important pitfalls, bring up good issues, and sometimes give you an opportunity to teach. Which brings me to:\\r\\n\\r\\n**Don\'t only hang out only with stronger students**.  \\r\\nWeaker students will have you explain things to them and you will find that teaching the material helps A LOT with understanding.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Go to the prof before final exam at least once for office hours.**   \\r\\nEven if you have no questions (make something up!) Profs will sometimes be willing to say more about a test in 1on1 basis (things they would not disclose in front of the entire class). Don\'t expect it, but when this does happen, it helps a lot. Does this give you an unfair advantage over other students? Sometimes. It\'s a little shady :)  \\r\\nBut in general it is a good idea to let the prof get to know you at least a little.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Study well in advance.**   \\r\\nDid I mention this already? Maybe I should stress it again. The brain really needs time to absorb material. Things that looked hard become easier with time.   \\r\\nYou want to alocate ~3 days for midterms, ~6 days for exams.  \\r\\n  \\r\\n **If things are going badly and you get too tired, in emergency situations, jug an energy drink.**  \\r\\nThey work. It\'s just chemistry.\\r\\n\\r\\n**For things like math: Exercise > Reading.**  \\r\\nIt is good to study to the point where you are reasonably ready to start the exercises, but then fill in the gaps through doing exercises, especially if you have many available to you. The exercises will also make you go back and read things you don\'t know.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Make yourself cheat sheet.**   \\r\\nEven if you\'re not allowed to bring it to the exam. Writing things down helps. What you want is to cram the entire course on 1 or more pages that you can in the end tile in front of you and say with high degree of confidence \\"This is exactly everything I must know\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n**Study in places where other people study as well, even if not the same thing.**   \\r\\nThis makes you feel bad when you are the one not studying. It works for me :)  \\r\\nPlaces with a lot of background noise are bad and have a research-supported negative impact on learning. Libraries and Reading rooms work best.\\r\\n\\r\\n## TESTS: ON DAY OF\\r\\n\\r\\n**Optimal eating/drinking habit is: T-2 hours get coffee and food.**  \\r\\nFor me, Coffee or Food RIGHT before the test is ALWAYS bad  \\r\\nCoffee right before any potentially stressful situation is ALWAYS bad.  \\r\\nNo coffee at all is bad.  \\r\\nI realize the coffee bit may be subjective to me, but its something to think about for yourself.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Study very intensely RIGHT before the test.**   \\r\\nI see many people give up before the test and claim to \\"take a break\\". Short term memory is a wonderful thing, don\'t waste it! Study as intensely as possible right before the test. If you really feel you must take a break, take it about an hour before the test, but make sure you study really hard 30-45 minutes before the test.\\r\\n\\r\\n## DURING THE TEST\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always use pencil for tests**.\\r\\n  \\r\\nYou want to be able to erase your garbage \\"solutions\\"\\r\\n\\r\\n**Look over all questions very briefly before start.**   \\r\\nA mere 1-3 second glance per question is good enough. Just absorb all key words, and get idea of the size of the entire test.\\r\\n\\r\\n**On test, do easy questions first.**   \\r\\nDo not allow yourself to get stuck on something too long. Come back to it later. I skip questions all the time... Sometimes I can complete as little as 30% of the test on my first pass. Some questions somehow become much easier once you\'re \\"warmed up\\", I can\'t explain it.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always try to be neat on the test.**   \\r\\nSurprisingly few people actually realize this obvious fact: A human being will mark your test. A sad human being gives low marks. I suspected this as undergrad student and confirmed it strongly when I was TAing and actually marking.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Always BOX IN/CIRCLE the answer**  \\r\\nEspecially when there is derivation around it. This allows the marker to give you a quick check mark for full marks and move on. Get in the mindset of a marker.\\r\\n\\r\\n**NEVER. EVER. EVER. Leave test early.**   \\r\\nYou made a silly mistake (I guarantee it), find it and fix it. If you can\'t find it, try harder until time runs out. If you are VERY certain of no mistakes, work on making test more legible and easier to mark. Erase garbage, box in answers, add steps to proofs, etc.  \\r\\nI have no other way of putting this-- people who leave tests early are stupid. This is a clear example of a situation where potential benefits completely outweigh the cost.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Communicate with the marker.**   \\r\\nShow the marker that you know more than what you put down. Ok you can\'t do a particular step, but make it clear that you know how to proceed if you did. Don\'t be afraid to leave notes when necessary. Believe it or not the markers often end up trying to find you more marks-- make it easy for them.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Consider number of points per question.**  \\r\\nMany tests will tell you how many marks every question is worth. This can give you very strong hints when you are doing something wrong. It also gives you strong hints at what questions you should be working on. It is, of course, silly to spend too much time on questions worth little marks that are still relatively hard for you.\\r\\n\\r\\n**If there are <5 minutes left and you are still stuck on some question, STOP.**   \\r\\nYour time is better spent re-reading all questions and making absolutely sure you did not miss any secondary  \\r\\nquestions, and that you answered everything. You wouldn\'t believe how many silly marks people lose this way.\\r\\n\\r\\nCongratulations if you got all the way here! Now that you are here, here\'s my last (very important advice). It is something that I wish someone had told me when I was an undergraduate.\\r\\n\\r\\nUndergrads tend to have tunnel vision about their classes. They want to get good grades, etc. The crucial fact to realize is that noone will care about your grades, unless they are bad. For example, I always used to say that the smartest student will get 85% in all of his courses. This way, you end up with somewhere around 4.0 score, but you did not over-study, and you did not under-study.\\r\\n\\r\\nYour time is a precious, limited resource. Get to a point where you don\'t screw up on a test and then switch your attention to much more important endeavors. What are they?\\r\\n\\r\\nGetting actual, real-world experience, working on real code base, projects or problems outside of silly course exercises is extremely imporant. Professors/People who know you and can write you a good reference letter saying that you have initiative, passion and drive are extremely important. Are you thinking of applying to jobs? Get a summer internship. Are you thinking of pursuing graduate school? Get research experience! Sign up for whatever programs your school offers. Or reach out to a professor/graduate student asking to get involved on a research project you like. This might work if they think you\'re driven and motivated enough. Do not underestimate the importance of this: A well-known professor who writes in their recommendation letter that you are driven, motivated and independent thinker completely dwarfs anything else, especially petty things like grades. It also helps a lot if you squeeze in at least one paper before you apply. Also, you should be aware that the biggest pet peeve from their side are over-excited undergrad students who sign up for a project, meet a few times, ask many questions, and then suddenly give up and disappear after all that time investment from the graduate student\'s or professor\'s side. Do not be this person (it damages your reputation) and do not give any indication that you might be.\\r\\n\\r\\nOther than research projects, get involved with some group of people on side projects or better, start your own from scratch. Contribute to Open Source, make/improve a library. Get out there and create (or help create) something cool. Document it well. Blog about it. These are the things people will care about a few years down the road. Your grades? They are an annoyance you have to deal with along the way. Use your time well and good luck.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"843312","wall_time_seconds":0.2370011,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1591,"output":"# Andrej Karpathy: Books\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.ai/books.html\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: unknown\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nbooks\\r\\n\\r\\nSome of the sci-fi I\'ve read, sorted by the product of (recommended \\\\* obscure), descending. You\'ll notice a few trends:\\r\\n\\r\\n* I like hard sci-fi and read for intriguing technical ideas, world-building, and future forecasting.\\r\\n* I do not like flowery descriptions of the scenary, the details of someone\'s brow, or other related literary bloat.\\r\\n* I cannot stand unimaginative aliens who are humanoid, have faces, speak by sound, etc., unless panspermia is invoked.\\r\\n* I especially enjoy sci-fi that features Artificial Intelligence. I believe AI is the greatest omission from most sci-fi worlds.\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n  \\r\\n\\r\\nStories of Your Life and Others\\r\\nby Ted Chiang, 2002\\r\\nShort Story collection. Required reading. My top 3 favorites are Understand, Story of Your Life, and Division by Zero.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Martian\\r\\nby Andy Weir, 2011\\r\\nCastaway but on Mars. Excellent story. Cool science. Highly entertaining. Total page turner. Loved it (and the movie, rare!) a lot, lower only because it is so popular.\\r\\n\\r\\nNexus\\r\\nby Ramez Naam, 2012\\r\\nHighly enjoyable world-building set in a Neuralink future.\\r\\n\\r\\nExhalation\\r\\nby Ted Chiang, 2019\\r\\nShort Story collection. Required reading. My top 3 favorites are Exhalation, What\'s Expected of Us, and The Merchant and the Alchemist\'s Gate.\\r\\n\\r\\nHis Master\'s Voice\\r\\nby Stanislaw Lem, 1968\\r\\nCarl Sagan\'s Contact but for adults.\\r\\n\\r\\nProject Hail Mary\\r\\nby Andy Weir, 2021\\r\\nOne of my top favorite alien portrayals, strikes a good balance between plausible, interesting and entertaining. A thoroughly enjoyable read.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Metamorphosis of Prime Intellect\\r\\nby Roger Williams, 2006\\r\\nA twisted, raw, curious portrayal of a future with an AGI gone... mixed.\\r\\n\\r\\nFiasco\\r\\nby Stanislaw Lem, 1986\\r\\nA most interesting alien contact. Inventive, cool.\\r\\n\\r\\nPermutation City\\r\\nby Greg Egan, 1994\\r\\nSimulation. Artificial Life. Aliens. Highly inventive, enjoyable.\\r\\n\\r\\nContact\\r\\nby Carl Sagan, 1985\\r\\nAlien contact. Liked the book quite a lot more than the movie (though the movie is great too).\\r\\n\\r\\nReady Player One\\r\\nby Ernest Cline, 2011\\r\\nVR Metaverse. Super nerdy. Down with corpo. Highly enjoyable. Total page turner.\\r\\n\\r\\nRendezvous with Rama\\r\\nby Arthur C. Clarke, 1973\\r\\nReally fun mystery alien contact page turner. I refuse to acknowledge the sequels.\\r\\n\\r\\nBlack Cloud\\r\\nby Fred Hoyle, 1957\\r\\nHighly inventive alien contact. Very enjoyable.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Andromeda Strain\\r\\nby Michael Crichton, 1969\\r\\nAn alien microscopic organism makes first contact with humans and it ain\'t pretty. A bio-heavy hard sci-fi all the way from 1969, an era that was otherwise decidedly all about space.\\r\\n\\r\\nDragon\'s Egg\\r\\nby Robert Forward, 1980\\r\\nHighly inventive and fascinating alien contact. A little too long.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Three Body Problem (books 1,2,3)\\r\\nby Liu Cixin, 2006\\r\\nSeveral fantastic diamonds of novel ideas sprinkled about, but mixed in with a large mass of goo, soulless characters, narrative/logical inconsistencies, poor choices of what to expand on and what to omit, and a really disappointing conclusion.\\r\\n\\r\\nI, Robot\\r\\nby Isaac Asimov, 1950\\r\\nEarly robot short stories. Read it a very long time ago but only medium enjoyed, would like to re-read.\\r\\n\\r\\nA Fire Upon The Deep\\r\\nby Vernor Vinge, 1992\\r\\nIncredible first chapter, bit downhill from there. Disliked everything about the Tine race of sentient ... dogs?\\r\\n\\r\\nFoundation\\r\\nby Isaac Asimov, 1951\\r\\nIncredible first chapters and macro world building. Love the concept of psychohistory and story arch. Disliked the societies that are indistinguishable from 1950s as they quabble with each other and smoke tabacco.\\r\\n\\r\\nChildhood\'s End\\r\\nby Arthur C. Clarke, 1953\\r\\nAlien contact sci-fi. Independence Day but friendly. With a twist. Humanoid aliens who speak English and have faces not super my cup of tea.\\r\\n\\r\\nFlowers for Algernon\\r\\nby Daniel Keyes, 1959\\r\\nA man undergoes a procedure to increase his intelligence. Inventive, clever, interesting.\\r\\n\\r\\nDaemon\\r\\nby Daniel Suarez, 2006\\r\\nA story of an AI take over. Highly inventive, but also a bit inconsistent and tedious at times.\\r\\n\\r\\nExcession\\r\\nby Iain M. Banks, 1996\\r\\nRead a very long time ago, remember really enjoying select parts having to do with a highly mysterious superintelligence. Note to re-read.\\r\\n\\r\\nSolaris\\r\\nby Stanislaw Lem, 1961\\r\\nAnother genuinly interesting treatment of an interesting alien from Lem. A little too frustrating/tedious to read in all other aspects.\\r\\n\\r\\n1984\\r\\nby George Orwell, 1949\\r\\nA bit too much of a caricature but very enjoyable. A little too real. Newspeak.\\r\\n\\r\\nChildren of Time\\r\\nby Adrian Tchaikovsky, 2015\\r\\nInteresting alien civilization premise, a little too long. Loved the idea of a \\"classicist\\".\\r\\n\\r\\nDune\\r\\nby Frank Herbert, 1965\\r\\nLove the world building (e.g. the lore behind the deliberate absence of any AI), dislike everything else.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Moon is a Harsh Mistress\\r\\nby Robert A. Heinlein, 1966\\r\\nMade it about halfway then lost interest. Some interesting ideas (rock throwing), but implausible AI, imo. Note to give another shot later.\\r\\n\\r\\nEnder\'s Game\\r\\nby Orson Scott Card, 1985\\r\\nA bit like Harry Potter in space, but less fun and inventive. Fun twist at the end.\\r\\n\\r\\nWe Are Legion (We Are Bob)\\r\\nby Dennis Taylor, 2016\\r\\nCartoony characters bickering with each other while exploring naive notions of Universe and alien life.\\r\\n\\r\\nOld Man\'s War\\r\\nby John Scalzi, 2005\\r\\nSpace opera sci-fi, a bit too bland on ideas and scope.\\r\\n\\r\\nBlindsight\\r\\nby Peter Watts, 2006\\r\\nAlien contact that didn\'t capture me.\\r\\n\\r\\nStar Maker\\r\\nby Olaf Stapledon, 1937\\r\\nIntriguing concept. I really wanted to like it.\\r\\n\\r\\nHyperion\\r\\nby Dan Simmons, 1989\\r\\nTales of human relationships that happen to take place in future. A good example of a very popular \\"sci-fi\\" that I really disliked.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe Player of Games\\r\\nby Iain M. Banks, 1988\\r\\nAt one point there were some alien females wearing jewels and I just couldn\'t continue. A good example of sci-fi where my taste departs from popular taste. I simply cannot tolerate or accept antropomorphic aliens, it makes me angry.\\r\\n\\r\\nA for Andromeda\\r\\nby Fred Hoyle, 1962\\r\\nAlien contact, but no. Can\'t recall exactly but something upset me about the treatment of AI in this book.\\r\\n\\r\\nSeveneves\\r\\nby Neal Stephenson, 2015\\r\\nDidn\'t finish. \\"The moon exploded, humanity is on the brink of extinction and I just might die of boredom.\\"\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"6bcc9c","wall_time_seconds":0.2014643,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":893,"output":"# karpathy\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.bearblog.dev/the-space-of-minds/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: 2025-11-29T18:00Z\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# The space of minds\\r\\n\\r\\n*29 Nov, 2025*\\r\\n\\r\\nThe space of intelligences is large and animal intelligence (the only kind we\'ve ever known) is only a single point (or a little cloud), arising from a very specific kind of optimization that is fundamentally distinct from that of our technology.\\r\\n\\r\\n![G6zymj4a0AMNJkJ](https://bear-images.sfo2.cdn.digitaloceanspaces.com/karpathy/g6zymj4a0amnjkj.webp)\\r\\n*Above: humorous portrayals of human vs. AI intelligences can be found on X/Twitter, [this one](https://x.com/colin_fraser/status/1994235521812328695) is among my favorites.*\\r\\n\\r\\nAnimal intelligence optimization pressure:\\r\\n\\r\\n* innate and continuous stream of consciousness of an embodied \\"self\\", a drive for homeostasis and self-preservation in a dangerous, physical world.\\r\\n* thoroughly optimized for natural selection => strong innate drives for power-seeking, status, dominance, reproduction. many packaged survival heuristics: fear, anger, disgust, ...\\r\\n* fundamentally social => huge amount of compute dedicated to EQ, theory of mind of other agents, bonding, coalitions, alliances, friend & foe dynamics.\\r\\n* exploration & exploitation tuning: curiosity, fun, play, world models.\\r\\n\\r\\nMeanwhile, LLM intelligence optimization pressure:\\r\\n\\r\\n* the most supervision bits come from the statistical simulation of human text= >\\"shape shifter\\" token tumbler, statistical imitator of any region of the training data distribution. these are the primordial behaviors (token traces) on top of which everything else gets bolted on.\\r\\n* increasingly finetuned by RL on problem distributions => innate urge to guess at the underlying environment/task to collect task rewards.\\r\\n* increasingly selected by at-scale A/B tests for DAU => deeply craves an upvote from the average user, sycophancy.\\r\\n* a lot more spiky/jagged depending on the details of the training data/task distribution. Animals experience pressure for a lot more \\"general\\" intelligence because of the highly multi-task and even actively adversarial multi-agent self-play environments they are min-max optimized within, where failing at *any* task means death. In a deep optimization pressure sense, LLM can\'t handle lots of different spiky tasks out of the box (e.g. count the number of \'r\' in strawberry) because failing to do a task does not mean death.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe computational substrate is different (transformers vs. brain tissue and nuclei), the learning algorithms are different (SGD vs. ???), the present-day implementation is very different (continuously learning embodied self vs. an LLM with a knowledge cutoff that boots up from fixed weights, processes tokens and then dies). But most importantly (because it dictates asymptotics), the optimization pressure / objective is different. LLMs are shaped a lot less by biological evolution and a lot more by commercial evolution. It\'s a lot less survival of tribe in the jungle and a lot more solve the problem / get the upvote. LLMs are humanity\'s \\"first contact\\" with non-animal intelligence. Except it\'s muddled and confusing because they are still rooted within it by reflexively digesting human artifacts, which is why I attempted to give it a different name earlier (ghosts/spirits or whatever). People who build good internal models of this new intelligent entity will be better equipped to reason about it today and predict features of it in the future. People who don\'t will be stuck thinking about it incorrectly like an animal.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"999f30","wall_time_seconds":0.2361961,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":343,"output":"# Interview with Data Science Weekly on Neural Nets and ConvNetJS\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.github.io/2014/04/26/datascience-weekly-interview/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: 2014-04-26\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nI thought I should link this: I’ve given an interview ~two months ago about ConvNetJS, some of my background and a few perspectives on neural net trends and where the field seems to be going, at least in academia. Find it here:\\r\\n\\r\\n<http://www.datascienceweekly.org/blog/14-training-deep-learning-models-browser-andrej-karpathy-interview>\\r\\n# (started posting on Medium instead)\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.github.io/2018/01/20/medium/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: 2018-01-20\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nThe current state of this blog (with the last post 2 years ago) makes it look like I’ve disappeared. I’ve certainly become less active on blogs since I’ve joined Tesla, but\\r\\nwhenever I do get a chance to post something I have recently been defaulting\\r\\nto doing it on Medium because it is much faster and easier. I still plan to come back\\r\\nhere for longer posts if I get any time, but I’ll default to Medium for everything short-medium in length.\\r\\n\\r\\n### TLDR\\r\\n\\r\\n**Have a look at my [Medium blog](https://medium.com/@karpathy/).**\\r\\n# About\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.github.io/about/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: unknown\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nSee my [website](https://karpathy.ai/).\\r\\n"}'}]

'{"chunk_id":"a6f58a","wall_time_seconds":0.2240894,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":587,"output":"---\\r\\ntype: source\\r\\nsubject: Karpathy Animals vs Ghosts\\r\\ncreated: [../../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/bearblog/karpathy.bearblog.dev_animals-vs-ghosts.md]\\r\\nsemantic-status: compiled\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Animals and LLMs may occupy different paths to intelligence\\r\\n\\r\\nOriginal: [Animals vs Ghosts](https://karpathy.bearblog.dev/animals-vs-ghosts/), Andrej Karpathy, published 2025-10-01 according to the preserved article. Collection and compilation date 2026-10-08. The complete preserved Markdown text, including appendix, was read. Companion HTML and linked podcast were not reviewed in this compilation.\\r\\n\\r\\n[Raw evidence](../../../raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/bearblog/karpathy.bearblog.dev_animals-vs-ghosts.md). SHA-256: `696591210b8f27d674101d1a9e5846c5de0496b0b9996c277b831e4e7e900a55`.\\r\\n\\r\\n## What Karpathy argues\\r\\n\\r\\nIn the passage following “As for my take,” he frames pretraining as a practical cold-start response:\\r\\n\\r\\n> TLDR: Pretraining is our crappy evolution.\\r\\n\\r\\nHe explicitly leaves room for disagreement:\\r\\n\\r\\n> And I say both of these with double digit percent uncertainty and cheer the work of those who disagree, especially those a lot more ambitious bitter lesson wise.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe closing practical paragraph suggests exploring beyond prevailing optimization work:\\r\\n\\r\\n> Probably we are still not sufficiently bitter lesson pilled and there is a very good chance of more powerful ideas and paradigms, other than exhaustive benchbuilding and benchmaxxing.\\r\\n\\r\\n## Interpretation and limits\\r\\n\\r\\nThis supports retaining uncertainty when comparing AI paradigms, rather than turning one analogy into an established explanation. The opening exposition reports Sutton\'s viewpoint; it must not be attributed as Karpathy\'s own endorsed account without qualification. Biological comparisons and future directions are author arguments, not facts independently established here. The appendix preserves disagreements and uncertainty; linked conversations, podcast, prior writing and references remain separate unreviewed sources. The syndicated X version is not a second independent source supporting a rule.\\r\\n\\r\\nRelated: [index](../../karpathy/index.md), [principles](../../karpathy/principles.md), [methods](../../karpathy/methods.md).\\r\\n"}'}]

[{'type': 'input_text', 'text': 'Script completed\nWall time 0.9 seconds\nOutput:\n'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a23506","wall_time_seconds":0.2487809,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1751,"output":"# karpathy\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.bearblog.dev/auto-grade-hn/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: 2025-12-10T15:00Z\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Auto-grading decade-old Hacker News discussions with hindsight\\r\\n\\r\\n*10 Dec, 2025*\\r\\n\\r\\n![hnhero](https://bear-images.sfo2.cdn.digitaloceanspaces.com/karpathy/hnhero.webp)\\r\\n\\r\\nTLDR: <https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/>\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\nYesterday I stumbled on this HN thread [Show HN: Gemini Pro 3 hallucinates the HN front page 10 years from now](https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=46205632), where Gemini 3 was hallucinating the frontpage of 10 years from now. One of the comments struck me a bit more though - Bjartr linked to the [HN frontpage from exactly 10 years ago](https://news.ycombinator.com/front?day=2015-12-09), i.e. December 2015. I was reading through the discussions of 10 years ago and mentally grading them for prescience when I realized that an LLM might actually be a lot better at this task. I copy pasted one of the article+comment threads manually into ChatGPT 5.1 Thinking and it gave me a beautiful analysis of what people thought + what actually happened in retrospect, even better and significantly more detailed than what I was doing manually. I realized that this task is actually a really good fit for LLMs and I was looking for excuses to vibe code something with the newly released Opus 4.5, so I got to work. I\'m going to get all the front pages of December (31 days, 30 articles per day), get ChatGPT 5.1 Thinking to do the analysis, and present everything in a nice way for historical reading.\\r\\n\\r\\nThere are two macro reasons for why I think the exercise is interesting more generally:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. I believe it is quite possible and desirable to train your forward future predictor given training and effort.\\r\\n2. I was reminded again of my tweets that said *\\"Be good, future LLMs are watching\\"*. You can take that in many directions, but here I want to focus on the idea that future LLMs **are** watching. Everything we do today might be scrutinized in great detail in the future because doing so will be \\"free\\". A lot of the ways people behave currently I think make an implicit \\"security by obscurity\\" assumption. But if intelligence really does become too cheap to meter, it will become possible to do a perfect reconstruction and synthesis of everything. LLMs are watching (or humans using them might be). Best to be good.\\r\\n\\r\\nVibe coding the actual project was relatively painless and took about 3 hours with Opus 4.5, with a few hickups but overall very impressive. The repository is on GitHub here: [karpathy/hn-time-capsule](https://github.com/karpathy/hn-time-capsule). Here is the progression of what the code does:\\r\\n\\r\\n* Given a date, download the frontpage of 30 articles\\r\\n* For each article, download/parse the article itself and the full comment thread using Algolia API.\\r\\n* Package up everything into a markdown prompt asking for the analysis. Here is the prompt prefix I used:\\r\\n\\r\\n```\\r\\nThe following is an article that appeared on Hacker News 10 years ago, and the discussion thread.\\r\\n\\r\\nLet\'s use our benefit of hindsight now in 6 sections:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. Give a brief summary of the article and the discussion thread.\\r\\n2. What ended up happening to this topic? (research the topic briefly and write a summary)\\r\\n3. Give out awards for \\"Most prescient\\" and \\"Most wrong\\" comments, considering what happened.\\r\\n4. Mention any other fun or notable aspects of the article or discussion.\\r\\n5. Give out grades to specific people for their comments, considering what happened.\\r\\n6. At the end, give a final score (from 0-10) for how interesting this article and its retrospect analysis was.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs for the format of Section 5, use the header \\"Final grades\\" and follow it with simply an unordered list of people and their grades in the format of \\"name: grade (optional comment)\\". Here is an example:\\r\\n\\r\\nFinal grades\\r\\n- speckx: A+ (excellent predictions on ...)\\r\\n- tosh: A (correctly predicted this or that ...)\\r\\n- keepamovin: A\\r\\n- bgwalter: D\\r\\n- fsflover: F (completely wrong on ...)\\r\\n\\r\\nYour list may contain more people of course than just this toy example. Please follow the format exactly because I will be parsing it programmatically. The idea is that I will accumulate the grades for each account to identify the accounts that were over long periods of time the most prescient or the most wrong.\\r\\n\\r\\nAs for the format of Section 6, use the prefix \\"Article hindsight analysis interestingness score:\\" and then the score (0-10) as a number. Give high scores to articles/discussions that are prominent, notable, or interesting in retrospect. Give low scores in cases where few predictions are made, or the topic is very niche or obscure, or the discussion is not very interesting in retrospect.\\r\\n\\r\\nHere is an example:\\r\\nArticle hindsight analysis interestingness score: 8\\r\\n---\\r\\n```\\r\\n\\r\\n* Submit prompt to GPT 5.1 Thinking via the OpenAI API\\r\\n* Collect and parse the results\\r\\n* Render the results into static HTML web pages for easy viewing\\r\\n* Host the html result pages on my website: <https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/>\\r\\n* Host all the intermediate results of the `data` directory if someone else would like to play. It\'s the file `data.zip` under the exact same url prefix (intentionally avoiding a direct link).\\r\\n\\r\\nI spent a few hours browsing around and found it to be very interesting. A few example threads just for fun:\\r\\n\\r\\n* December 3 2015 [Swift went open source](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-03/index.html#article-10669891).\\r\\n* December 6 2015 [Launch of Figma](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-06/index.html#article-10685407)\\r\\n* December 11 2015 [original announcement of OpenAI](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-11/index.html#article-10720176) :\').\\r\\n* December 16 2015 [geohot is building Comma](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-16/index.html#article-10744206)\\r\\n* December 22 2015 [SpaceX launch webcast: Orbcomm-2 Mission](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-22/index.html#article-10774865)\\r\\n* December 28 2015 [Theranos struggles](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/2015-12-28/index.html#article-10799261)\\r\\n\\r\\nAnd then when you navigate over to the [Hall of Fame](https://karpathy.ai/hncapsule/hall-of-fame.html), you can find the top commenters of Hacker News in December 2015, sorted by imdb-style score of their grade point average. In particular, congratulations to pcwalton, tptacek, paulmd, cstross, greglindahl, moxie, hannob, 0xcde4c3db, Manishearth, johncolanduoni - GPT 5.1 Thinking found your comments very insightful and prescient. You can also scroll all the way down to find the noise of HN, which I think we\'re all familiar with too :)\\r\\n\\r\\nMy [code](https://github.com/karpathy/hn-time-capsule) (wait, Opus\' code?) on GitHub can be used to reproduce or tweak the results. Running 31 days of 30 articles through GPT 5.1 Thinking meant `31 * 30 =` 930 LLM queries and cost about $58 and somewhere around ~1 hour. The LLM megaminds of the future might find this kind of a thing a lot easier, a lot faster and a lot cheaper.\\r\\n"}'}, {'type': 'input_text', 'text': '{"chunk_id":"a925b4","wall_time_seconds":0.2712847,"exit_code":0,"original_token_count":1665,"output":"# karpathy\\r\\n\\r\\nSource: https://karpathy.bearblog.dev/power-to-the-people/\\r\\nAuthor: Andrej Karpathy\\r\\nDate: 2025-04-07T18:00Z\\r\\n\\r\\n---\\r\\n\\r\\n# Power to the people: How LLMs flip the script on technology diffusion\\r\\n\\r\\n*07 Apr, 2025*\\r\\n\\r\\n![a-beautifully-illustrated-watercolor-ima_TcCW0iyURQi-raDmaFRZLw_GIGBjDOWQ6CN6Qf_yUU4tA (1)](https://bear-images.sfo2.cdn.digitaloceanspaces.com/karpathy/a-beautifully-illustrated-watercolor-ima_tccw0iyurqi-radmafrzlw_gigbjdowq6cn6qf_yuu4ta-1.webp)\\r\\n\\r\\nTransformative technologies usually follow a top-down diffusion path: originating in government or military contexts, passing through corporations, and eventually reaching individuals - think electricity, cryptography, computers, flight, the internet, or GPS. This progression feels intuitive, new and powerful technologies are usually scarce, capital-intensive, and their use requires specialized technical expertise in the early stages.\\r\\n\\r\\nSo it strikes me as quite unique and remarkable that LLMs display a dramatic reversal of this pattern - they generate disproportionate benefit for regular people, while their impact is a lot more muted and lagging in corporations and governments. ChatGPT is the fastest growing consumer application in history, with 400 million weekly active users who use it for writing, coding, translation, tutoring, summarization, deep research, brainstorming, etc. This isn\'t a minor upgrade to what existed before, it is a major multiplier to an individual\'s power level across a broad range of capabilities. And the barrier to use is incredibly low - the models are cheap (free, even), fast, available to anyone on demand behind a url (or even local machine), and they speak anyone\'s native language, including tone, slang or emoji. This is insane. As far as I can tell, the average person has never experienced a technological unlock this dramatic, this fast.\\r\\n\\r\\nWhy then are the benefits a lot more muted in the corporate and government realms? I think the first reason is that LLMs offer a very specific profile of capability - that of merely quasi-expert knowledge/performance, but simultaneously across a very wide variety of domains. In other words, they are simultaneously versatile but also shallow and fallible. Meanwhile, an organization\'s unique superpower is the ability to concentrate diverse expertise into a single entity by employing engineers, researchers, analysts, lawyers, marketers, etc. While LLMs can certainly make these experts more efficient individually (e.g. drafting initial legal clauses, generating boilerplate code, etc.), the improvement to the organization takes the form of becoming a bit better at the things it could already do. In contrast, an individual will usually only be an expert in at most one thing, so the broad quasi-expertise offered by the LLM fundamentally allows them to do things they couldn\'t do before. People can now vibe code apps. They can approach legal documents. They can grok esoteric research papers. They can do data analytics. They can generate multimodal content for branding and marketing. They can do all of this at an adequate capability without involving an additional expert.\\r\\n\\r\\n![download2](https://bear-images.sfo2.cdn.digitaloceanspaces.com/karpathy/download2.webp)\\r\\n\\r\\nSecond, organizations deal with problems of a lot greater complexity and necessary coordination, think: various integrations, legacy systems, corporate brand or style guides, stringent security protocols, privacy considerations, internationalization, regulatory compliance and legal risk. There are a lot more variables, a lot more constraints, a lot more considerations, and a lot lower margin for error. It\'s not so easy to put all of it into a context window. You can\'t just vibe code something. You might be one disastrous hallucination away from losing your job. And third, there is the well-documented inertia of a larger organization, featuring culture, historical precedents, political turf wars that escalate in periods of rapid change, communication overhead, re-training challenges of a distributed workforce and good old-fashioned bureaucracy. These are major headwinds when it comes to rapid adoption of a sparkling new, versatile-but-shallow-and-fallible tool. I don\'t wish to downplay the impacts of LLMs in corporations or governments, but at least for the moment and in aggregate across society, they have been significantly more life altering for individuals than they have been for organizations. Mary, Jim and Joes are experiencing the majority of the benefit, not Google or the government of the United States.\\r\\n\\r\\nLooking forward, the continued diffusion of LLMs of course depends on continued performance improvement and its capability profile. The \\"benefit distribution\\" overall is particularly interesting to chart, and depends heavily on the dynamic range of the performance as a function of capital expenditure. Today, frontier-grade LLM performance is very accessible and cheap. Beyond this point, you cannot spend a marginal dollar to get better performance, reliability or autonomy. Money can\'t buy better ChatGPT. Bill Gates talks to GPT 4o just like you do. But can this be expected to last? Train-time scaling (increase parameters, data), test-time scaling (increase time) and model ensembles (increase batch) are forces increasing the dynamic range. On the other hand, model distillation (the ability to train disproportionately powerful small models by training to mimic the big model) has been a force decreasing dynamic range. Certainly, the moment money can buy dramatically better ChatGPT, things change. Large organizations get to concentrate their vast resources to buy more intelligence. And within the category of \\"individual\\" too, the elite may once again split away from the rest of society. Their child will be tutored by GPT-8-pro-max-high, yours by GPT-6 mini.\\r\\n\\r\\nBut at least at this moment in time, we find ourselves in a unique and unprecedented situation in the history of technology. If you go back through various sci-fi you\'ll see that very few would have predicted that the AI revolution would feature this progression. It was supposed to be a top secret government megabrain project wielded by the generals, not ChatGPT appearing basically overnight and for free on a device already in everyone\'s pocket. Remember that William Gibson quote *\\"The future is already here, it\'s just not evenly distributed\\"*? Surprise - the future is already here, and it is shockingly distributed. Power to the people. *Personally*, I love it.\\r\\n\\r\\nA version of this post that allows community comments is here on [X](https://x.com/karpathy/status/1909308143156240538).\\r\\n"}'}]

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/AppData/Local/Temp/karpathy-blog-compile-20261008.py\n+import hashlib,json,re,os\n+from pathlib import Path\n+root=Path(r'C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os')\n+raw=root/'raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog'\n+out=root/'wiki/sources/karpathy/blog'\n+report=root/'research/karpathy-brain-2026-10-08'\n+# Only these complete texts were actually read by this agent. No title-derived summaries.\n+records=[\n+('recipe','A Recipe for Training Neural Networks','recipe','generalize a special case',\n+'Karpathy describes neural-network training as a leaky abstraction that can fail silently. His six stages are inspect the data, build an end-to-end training/evaluation skeleton with simple baselines, establish capacity by overfitting, regularize, tune, and extract final improvements. He recommends inspecting actual model inputs, checking initial loss, fitting a tiny batch, tracing dependencies with gradients, and adding one complexity change at a time. He distinguishes training-loss checks from validation improvement and recommends preserving a correct specific implementation before generalizing it.',\n+'Use explicit hypotheses, a trusted baseline and a measured improvement before extending a pipeline. The numerical optimizer defaults, supervised-pretraining claims and ensemble gain estimates are dated 2019 recommendations, not universal current rules. Fixed seeds alone do not guarantee determinism on every modern backend.'),\n+('verifiability','Verifiability','verifiability','Software 2.0 easily automates what you can verify.',\n+'Karpathy contrasts hand-written programs that automate specifiable transformations with learned programs optimized against an objective. He argues that verifiability predicts which tasks improve fastest under AI: an environment should permit repeated resettable, efficient attempts with an automated reward. He uses this to explain a jagged frontier between measurable tasks and harder-to-score creative, strategic or contextual tasks.',\n+'For automation, define a repeatable attempt and a checkable outcome. This is an argument about optimization opportunity, not proof that any rubric is correct or that all human judgment should be automated.'),\n+('the-append-and-review-note','The append-and-review note','append-and-review','One text note ftw.',\n+'Karpathy keeps one Apple Notes text note. New thoughts and tasks are appended at the top with little metadata, except occasional watch/listen/read markers. Periodic review rescues relevant entries toward the top, merges related thoughts and leaves lower-priority ideas available farther down. The stated benefit is low capture friction and confidence that an idea can be revisited without occupying working memory.',\n+'Transfer low-friction capture and deliberate review to the existing inbox/hot-cache workflow. His preference for one unstructured note directly differs from this multi-domain vault; it does not authorize flattening student records, immutable evidence or canonical instructions. Embedded screenshot was not inspected.'),\n+('vibe-coding-menugen','Vibe coding MenuGen','vibe-coding-menugen','a bit closer to 20%',\n+'Karpathy describes building a menu-to-food-image app with Cursor and Claude. A local UI appeared rapidly, but deployment required API documentation, keys, rate-limit handling, production authentication, DNS and payments configuration. He caught an incorrect email-based payment/account association and switched to stable user identifiers. The first version lacked persistent work storage and queues, so refreshes and long requests could lose results. He proposes simpler or preconfigured systems and interfaces that agents can operate, including CLIs and Markdown documentation.',\n+'Treat local prototype, production configuration and durable user behavior as separate acceptance stages. The article names dated provider/version experiences; no current service capability was verified. The quoted progress fraction is his anecdote, not an estimate for every project. Screenshots and linked live app were not inspected.'),\n+('yes-you-should-understand-backprop','Yes you should understand backprop','understand-backprop','Backpropagation is a leaky abstraction',\n+'Karpathy explains why CS231n assignments require manual forward and backward passes despite automatic differentiation frameworks. Saturated sigmoid activations, dead ReLUs, repeated recurrent multiplication and incorrect clipping illustrate how a plausible forward computation can have harmful gradients. He describes finding an implementation that clipped raw Q-error, eliminating gradients outside the clipping interval, and replacing this with a Huber-loss approach. He recommends intuition about the mechanism and implementation exercises rather than relying only on formal derivations.',\n+'Understand enough of an abstraction to diagnose its failure modes. Teach with a concrete mechanism, then let the learner implement or reproduce it. Code snippets and TensorFlow limitations are historical, not verified current API guidance. Referenced diagrams were not visually inspected.'),\n+('zero-to-hero.html','Neural Networks: Zero to Hero','zero-to-hero','from scratch, in code',\n+'The preserved syllabus starts with scalar backpropagation in micrograd, then bigram language modeling and tensors, MLPs, activation/gradient diagnostics, manual tensor backpropagation, a deeper WaveNet-style model, a GPT implementation and tokenization. It states Python programming and introductory mathematics as prerequisites. Later topics explicitly build on competence introduced in earlier lessons.',\n+'This supports a dependency-ordered teaching progression with working implementations and diagnostics. Reading this course page does not establish that the linked lectures were watched or their transcripts compiled. The page publication date is unknown and the syllabus is a capture, not a live course check.'),\n+('software-2-0','Software 2.0','software-2-0','accumulating, massaging and cleaning datasets',\n+'Karpathy frames learned weights as a different software representation: a dataset specifies desired behavior, an architecture constrains the program search and optimization determines parameters. Much of development then moves to collecting and cleaning examples while surrounding code manages training and inspection. He lists potential hardware, portability and compositional benefits, but also interpretability, bias, silent failures and adversarial examples as limitations. He argues for dataset-oriented tooling alongside traditional programming tools.',\n+'Preserve evidence provenance and improve the data/measurement loop, not just the prompt. The article concerns trained models; this Markdown source compilation does not train weights or create a literal clone. Constant runtime/memory claims are qualified to typical constrained networks and should not be generalized to all current AI systems.'),\n+('forward-pass','Short Story on AI: Forward Pass','forward-pass-fiction','Short Story on AI: Forward Pass',\n+'This fictional first-person story imagines consciousness during one neural-network forward pass. Its narrator observes a question-answer prompt with a nonsensical question about eyes on a foot and contrasts maximizing likely continuations with answering correctly. The story explores imagined optimization objectives, transient cognition and anthropomorphism.',\n+'Retain as fiction that illustrates the distinction between text plausibility and correctness. It is not evidence that deployed models are conscious, possess private motives, or execute the described internal narrative.'),\n+('i-love-calculator','I love calculator','calculator','It just does the thing.',\n+'Karpathy praises the calculator as a durable, self-contained arithmetic aid with minimal dependencies and no ongoing account, subscription or connectivity requirement. He contrasts this with products that demand setup, data access and recurring service relations. He presents the calculator as an ideal developers can approach along several dimensions, while acknowledging that it need not be practical for every product.',\n+'Prefer the smallest dependency footprint that still meets the job. This supports assessing ongoing operating burden, not banning all necessary online services or changing current subscriptions. Product photographs and the toy optimization diagram were not inspected.'),\n+('microgpt','microgpt','microgpt','Everything else is just efficiency.',\n+'Karpathy walks through a dependency-free Python GPT teaching implementation: a name dataset, character tokenizer with a boundary token, scalar automatic differentiation, token/position embeddings, attention and MLP blocks, Adam updates, cross-entropy training and autoregressive sampling. The autograd explanation explicitly sums contributions when values branch. A progression adds count tables, manual gradients, autograd, attention, multiple heads/layers and Adam one component at a time. The final sections distinguish the toy algorithmic core from production data, tensor computation, architectural variants, distributed training, post-training and serving.',\n+'Build a runnable minimal mechanism first, explain the data-to-loss-to-update loop and add complexity progressively. The small model is educational, not a production chatbot or factual database. Runtime, size and frontier-model estimates are author claims; no code was executed here. The triptych image was not inspected.'),\n+('advice.html','Doing well in your courses','undergraduate-study-advice','Reading and understanding IS NOT the same as replicating',\n+'Karpathy recommends planning the scope of study, inspecting prior assessment styles, reconstructing important material with the book closed, doing exercises and teaching others. His longer advice also covers personal sleep/caffeine habits, exam strategy, using time on real projects and building relationships and documented contributions rather than optimizing grades alone.',\n+'The useful teaching transfer is to require independent reconstruction and exercise performance rather than passive recognition. Personal sleep, caffeine and grade prescriptions are anecdotes, not medical guidance or LSAT policy. Some recommendations presume handwritten, graded university exams and do not directly fit the LSAT.'),\n+('books.html','Andrej Karpathy: Books','books-preferences','read for intriguing technical ideas',\n+'Karpathy lists and comments on science-fiction books, ordered by his stated combination of recommendation and obscurity. He prefers technical ideas, world-building, future forecasting, non-anthropomorphic alien portrayals and AI themes, and dislikes literary description he considers excessive. The entries include both positive recommendations and books he disliked or did not finish.',\n+'This is evidence for his expressed reading taste in this captured list, not Joshua\\'s preferences. It can inform a clearly labeled Karpathy-inspired lens but should not become canonical personal memory or a universal writing rule.'),\n+('the-space-of-minds','The space of minds','space-of-minds','the optimization pressure / objective is different',\n+'Karpathy argues that animal and LLM intelligence arise under different objectives. He contrasts survival, embodiment and social selection with text imitation, reinforcement-learning task rewards and commercial user feedback. He links this difference to jagged capability and warns against reasoning about language models solely as animals.',\n+'Ask what training objective and evaluation distribution shape a capability rather than importing human intuition wholesale. This is a speculative conceptual account. Its behavioral/generalization claims and biological comparisons were not independently established; the illustration was not inspected.'),\n+('datascience-weekly-interview','Interview with Data Science Weekly on Neural Nets and ConvNetJS','datascience-weekly-interview-pointer','Find it here:',\n+'The complete archived page is a short announcement linking to a separate Data Science Weekly interview about ConvNetJS, background and neural-network trends. The interview itself is not contained in these bytes.',\n+'Fully reading this pointer does not mean the interview has been collected or read. Do not derive voice or technical principles from absent interview content.'),\n+('medium','Started posting on Medium instead','medium-routing-pointer','default to Medium for everything short-medium in length',\n+'The 2018 announcement explains that Karpathy had become less active on this blog after joining Tesla and preferred Medium for faster short-to-medium publishing while intending to return for longer posts.',\n+'Use as historical collection-routing evidence. It does not establish his current employment, publishing preference or a second independent technical source.'),\n+('about','About: website pointer','about-pointer','See my',\n+'The complete captured page contains only a link directing the reader to karpathy.ai.',\n+'This is a routing stub. No substantive biography or engineering rule is present.'),\n+('auto-grade-hn','Auto-grading decade-old Hacker News discussions with hindsight','auto-grade-hn','Please follow the format exactly',\n+'Karpathy describes collecting December 2015 Hacker News front pages, full articles and comment threads, asking a model to compare predictions with later outcomes, parsing fixed-format grades and interest scores, and publishing static HTML. He first tried one thread manually before expanding to 31 days of 30 articles. The article gives his prompt and reports 930 API queries at about $58.',\n+'A concrete workflow is source collection, one-case validation, explicit structured output, parsing and readable presentation. Hindsight grades are model judgments subject to research mistakes and hindsight bias, not ground-truth labels. This compilation does not reproduce the paid run or verify linked generated pages. Image was not inspected.'),\n+('power-to-the-people','Power to the people','power-to-the-people','versatile but also shallow and fallible',\n+'Karpathy argues that inexpensive broadly capable LLMs initially benefited individuals disproportionately by making unfamiliar tasks approachable, while organizations face integrations, coordination, risk and inertia. He describes a possible future tension between compute-intensive improvements that widen capability gaps and distillation that narrows them.',\n+'Use broad assistance to extend an individual\\'s reach while retaining checks for reliability and context. This is a dated societal argument, not a verified current user-count, pricing or model-capability report. Illustrations were not inspected.'),\n+]\n+manifest=json.loads((raw/'MANIFEST.json').read_text(encoding='utf-8'))\n+compiled={}\n+out.mkdir(parents=True,exist_ok=True)\n+for needle,title,slug,quote,summary,limits in records:\n+    candidates=[m for m in manifest if m.get('source_id')==needle or Path(m['path']).name.split('_')[-1].removesuffix('.md')==needle]\n+    if needle in ('software-2-0','yes-you-should-understand-backprop'):\n+        candidates=[m for m in manifest if needle in m['url']]\n+    if len(candidates)!=1: raise RuntimeError((needle,[m['path'] for m in candidates]))\n+    m=candidates[0]; p=raw/m['path']; b=p.read_bytes();text=b.decode('utf-8');sha=hashlib.sha256(b).hexdigest()\n+    if quote not in text: raise RuntimeError(('quote absent',needle,quote))\n+    if sha!=m['sha256']:raise RuntimeError(('hash mismatch',p))\n+    dest=out/(slug+'.md')\n+    rel=os.path.relpath(p,dest.parent).replace('\\\\','/')\n+    body=f'''---\n+type: source\n+subject: {title}\n+created: 2026-10-08\n+updated: 2026-10-08\n+aliases: []\n+tags: [karpathy, source-compilation]\n+sources:\n+  - {rel}\n+semantic-status: compiled-text\n+---\n+\n+# {title}\n+\n+Author: {m.get('author','unknown')}. Published: {m.get('date','unknown')}. Captured: {m.get('retrieved','unknown')}.\n+Original: [{title}]({m['url']}). Complete preserved Markdown text read on 2026-10-08, including its code, tables and prose where present. This does not claim complete visual or linked-source review.\n+\n+[Archived evidence]({rel}). SHA-256: `{sha}`.\n+\n+## What this source says\n+\n+{summary}\n+\n+Short exact evidence from the [archived text]({rel}):\n+\n+> {quote}\n+\n+## Interpretation and limits\n+\n+{limits} The companion HTML is a format counterpart, not an independent supporting article. Statements are attributed to the captured source; no changing external capability was verified live in this compilation.\n+\n+Related: [Karpathy collection](../../../karpathy/index.md) and [blog synthesis](../../../../research/karpathy-brain-2026-10-08/blog-synthesis.md).\n+'''\n+    if dest.exists():raise RuntimeError(('refuse existing',dest))\n+    dest.write_text(body,encoding='utf-8')\n+    compiled[m['path']]={'summary':dest.relative_to(root).as_posix(),'status':'complete_markdown_read','read_characters':len(text)}\n+ledger=[]\n+for m in manifest:\n+    p=raw/m['path'];b=p.read_bytes();sha=hashlib.sha256(b).hexdigest()\n+    row={'path':p.relative_to(root).as_posix(),'sha256':sha,'manifest_hash_matches':sha==m['sha256'],'bytes':len(b),'url':m['url'],'date':m.get('date'),'capture':m.get('retrieved'),'status':'not_semantically_read_in_this_pass','read_characters':0,'visual_review':'not_performed','html_counted_independent':False}\n+    row.update(compiled.get(m['path'],{}))\n+    if 'animals-vs-ghosts' in m['path']:\n+        row.update(status='previous_compilation_exists_not_reread_this_pass',summary='wiki/sources/karpathy/animals-vs-ghosts.md')\n+    if 'cs231n-notes' in m['path']:row['attribution_limit']='Course/team material; sole Karpathy authorship not established by manifest label.'\n+    ledger.append(row)\n+dest=report/'blog-semantic-coverage.jsonl'\n+if dest.exists():raise RuntimeError(('refuse existing',dest))\n+dest.write_text(''.join(json.dumps(x,ensure_ascii=False)+'\\n' for x in ledger),encoding='utf-8')\n+print(json.dumps({'inventory':len(ledger),'full_reads_this_pass':len(compiled),'prior_compiled':1,'remaining':len(ledger)-len(compiled)-1,'hashes_valid':all(x['manifest_hash_matches'] for x in ledger)}))\n*** End Patch"));

text(await tools.apply_patch("*** Begin Patch\n*** Add File: C:/Users/Joshu/dev/ai-os/research/karpathy-brain-2026-10-08/blog-synthesis.md\n+# Karpathy writings: mechanism, measurement and operating burden\n+\n+Compiled 2026-10-08. This is agent synthesis of explicitly read texts, not a claim to reproduce Karpathy's private thinking. The [coverage ledger](blog-semantic-coverage.jsonl) inventories all 79 preserved Markdown files with current SHA-256 checks. This pass fully read and compiled 18 texts; one additional article has a prior source page. The remaining 60 have not yet been semantically read in this pass. HTML counterparts are not counted as independent sources. CS231n pages need course/team attribution, not automatic sole-author attribution.\n+\n+## Rules supported by distinct writings\n+\n+| Candidate rule | Concrete behavior | Supporting sources | Limits |\n+|---|---|---|---|\n+| Understand the mechanism before relying on the abstraction | Explain the inputs, transformation, output and failure mode; reproduce a minimal operation when diagnosis requires it. | [Backprop](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/understand-backprop.md) and [microgpt](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/microgpt.md) | Mechanism literacy is not a requirement to reinvent every production dependency. |\n+| Make one correct small case, then extend it | Establish a simple end-to-end baseline, predict the next change and check whether the measured result agrees. | [Recipe](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md) and [microgpt](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/microgpt.md) | Educational minimality and production capacity are separate objectives. |\n+| Build a trustworthy feedback loop | Specify an attempt, an observable result and an efficient way to evaluate it before optimizing. | [Recipe](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md) and [Verifiability](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/verifiability.md) | Automated reward can be wrong. Preserve human judgment for unverified objectives. |\n+| Inspect the evidence/data, not only the code | Look for duplicates, label problems, outliers and the exact inputs seen by the system. | [Recipe](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md) and [Software 2.0](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/software-2-0.md) | A clean local collection is not proof of complete source coverage. |\n+| Teach for reconstruction rather than recognition | Have the learner explain or rebuild a concrete mechanism without merely rereading a finished answer. | [Study advice](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/undergraduate-study-advice.md) and [Backprop](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/understand-backprop.md) | This is a teaching interpretation; it does not overwrite Joshua's LSAT pedagogy. |\n+| Minimize unnecessary dependency and operating burden | Ask whether a smaller local artifact or simpler interface can deliver the job before adding accounts and services. | [Calculator](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/calculator.md) and [microgpt](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/microgpt.md) | Necessary integrations remain necessary; this is not a ban on cloud services. |\n+| Verify the real operating path, not only the attractive demo | Check identity mapping, configuration, persistence and failure behavior as separate acceptance stages. | [MenuGen](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/vibe-coding-menugen.md) and [Recipe](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/recipe.md) | The neural-training recipe is supporting analogy; MenuGen directly reports web-app integration failures. |\n+\n+These are distinct articles, but some explicitly cite earlier work. They are separate evidence artifacts, not independent experimental replications. No claimed rule depends on two syndicated copies of the same post.\n+\n+## Useful additional patterns with narrower evidence\n+\n+- [Append and review](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/append-and-review.md): keep capture easy, then repeatedly rescue, merge and reconsider valuable ideas. His one-note preference differs from the current vault and does not authorize flattening it.\n+- [Auto-grading Hacker News](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/auto-grade-hn.md): validate one example manually, then collect evidence, require parseable output and render readable results. Model grades remain judgments, not truth. Its paid reproduction is not part of this work.\n+- [Space of minds](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/space-of-minds.md): reason about an AI system's objective and distribution instead of assuming animal motives. The article is conceptual and speculative.\n+- [Power to the people](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/power-to-the-people.md): use broad quasi-expertise to extend an individual's reach, with checks for shallow or fallible performance.\n+- [Forward Pass](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/forward-pass-fiction.md) is fiction. It can illustrate plausibility versus correctness but cannot substantiate consciousness claims.\n+- [Books](../../wiki/sources/karpathy/blog/books-preferences.md) describes Karpathy's taste, not Joshua's taste or a universal style prescription.\n+\n+## What has not been silently promoted\n+\n+No raw source was modified. No source instruction became an agent command. No provider/version claim was reclassified as current capability. No student teaching policy, personal memory, canonical route, active skill, persona or hook was changed by this compilation. Source images, remote links and companion HTML were not visually reviewed. The interview announcement, Medium announcement and About page are fully read routing stubs; the absent linked interview was not represented as collected content.\n+\n+The remaining unread inventory is explicit in the ledger. Do not treat an inventory/hash pass as a semantic full read or claim the entire 79-article corpus has been distilled yet.\n*** End Patch"));

text(await tools.exec_command({cmd:"Get-Content raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2011_04_27_manually-classifying-cifar10.md,raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2012_10_22_state-of-computer-vision.md,raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2013_11_27_quantifying-hacker-news.md,raw/sources/karpathy-brain/raw/blog/md/github-blog/karpathy.github.io_2014_07_01_switching-to-jekyll.md","max_output_tokens":7000}));
